# CUB top-k knowledge base

What has been learned about making CCCL's top-k implementations faster, where the evidence lives, and what is still open. Hub document for Claude Code sessions and for people. Maintainer: Elias Stehle (estehle@nvidia.com) with Claude Code sessions.

- Source of truth: this file is the branch copy (`dsb_experiments/topk_swarm/KNOWLEDGE_BASE.md` on `exp/topk-primitives-swarm`) of the maintainer's master document, which lives outside the repository and is refreshed here at each version.
- This is the branch copy of the knowledge base (`dsb_experiments/topk_swarm/KNOWLEDGE_BASE.md`), the master file lives outside the repository and a private rendered copy is regenerated from it (section 9), neither is linked from here.
- Last updated 2026-10-25. Ledger of changes in section 8.

**If you are a Claude Code session starting top-k work:** read sections 1 and 2 in full, then the section for the implementation you touch (3 cluster agent, 4 block top-k, 5 multi-CTA, 10 the warp and block primitives swarm study, 11 to 15 its wave 1 approaches W1, W2, B1, B2 and B3, 16 the measured winners, 17 to 23 the wave 2 approaches N8, W5 with N7, N10, N5, B4 with N9, N6 at warp scope with W3 and N1, and W4), then open the reports listed in section 7 for the detail you need. Before you finish, follow section 9 (ledger entry, status board, re-render). Sections 6 to 9 are the housekeeping sections and keep their numbers, new study and approach sections are appended from section 10 on.

## 1. Landscape

### 1.1 The implementations

| Implementation | Where | What it is | State |
|---|---|---|---|
| `cub::DeviceTopK` | `cub/cub/device/device_topk.cuh`, `agent/agent_topk.cuh`, `dispatch/dispatch_topk.cuh`, `dispatch/tuning/tuning_topk.cuh` | Radix select over one array, several passes of histogram plus candidate filtering. The per-key radix functors `extract_bin_op_t` and `identify_candidates_op_t` in `dispatch_topk.cuh` are shared with the batched cluster agent, so a twiddle change there affects both. PR #8299 split the first histogram pass into its own kernel, PR #10627 fixed a race. | shipped |
| `cub::DeviceBatchedTopK`, baseline backend | `agent/agent_batched_topk.cuh`, `dispatch/dispatch_batched_topk.cuh`, `dispatch/kernels/kernel_batched_topk.cuh`, `dispatch/tuning/tuning_batched_topk.cuh` | One block per segment, built on the block top-k (section 4). Covers a segment only while it fits the block's shared memory. Non-deterministic only. | shipped |
| `cub::DeviceBatchedTopK`, cluster backend | `agent/agent_batched_topk_cluster.cuh` (PR #9224, SM 9.0 and later) | A thread-block cluster per segment, radix select with per-pass histograms reduced across the cluster through distributed shared memory and cluster barriers, resident chunks plus a streamed overflow, a deterministic final filter with index tie-break and a cheaper non-deterministic filter. The only backend that honors a determinism or tie-break request. | shipped, the subject of section 3 |
| Backend selector | `policy_selector_from_types` in `dispatch_batched_topk.cuh` | A deterministic or explicit tie-break request always goes to the cluster backend. Otherwise the baseline backend runs when it can cover the static max segment size and that size is below `cluster_beneficial_min_segment_size` (8K keys, `tuning_batched_topk.cuh`), the cluster backend above. A `tune` environment override can pin either backend. | shipped |
| `cub::BlockTopK` | `cub/cub/block/block_topk.cuh`, radix specialization `block/specializations/block_topk_air.cuh` (PR #7384, latency work in PR #11060) | Block-level radix select ("AIR"), the building block of the baseline backend and usable on its own. | shipped, the subject of section 4 |
| `cub::WarpBitonicTopK` | `cub/cub/warp/warp_bitonic_topk.cuh` | Warp-level bitonic top-k (eager form), benchmark `bench/bitonic_sort/warp_topk_pairs.cu`. PR #9281 holds the buffered variant (threshold plus candidate compaction). | open PR #12222 (not merged as of 2026-10-25), cherry-picked onto the swarm branch, the W1 reference primitive of the swarm study (section 10) |
| Multi-CTA batched top-k | no code yet | Several CTAs per segment cooperating without clusters (global memory, grid-level sync or decoupled passes), the planned middle ground between the one-block baseline and the cluster agent. | planned, section 5 |

### 1.2 Requirements API

`cuda::execution::determinism` (`not_guaranteed`, `gpu_to_gpu`), `cuda::execution::tie_break` (`prefer_smaller_index`, `prefer_larger_index`, unspecified) and `cuda::execution::output_ordering` (`sorted`, `unsorted`) travel in the environment (PRs #9238, #5781, #9331, docs #9446). The cluster agent derives `normalize_minus_zero = TieBreak != unspecified` (its line 255), which is why the signed-zero work in section 3 only touches the two index tie-breaks. PR #12063 makes `DeviceTopK` reject tie-break requests it cannot serve.

### 1.3 Benchmarks

`cub/benchmarks/bench/segmented_topk/`: `fixed/keys.cu` (fixed-size segments, throughput) and `variable/{keys,indexed}{,.cluster}.cu` with shared `keys_common.cuh`, `indexed_common.cuh`, `common.cuh`. The `.cluster` translation units default to `TUNE_REQUIREMENT 1` (deterministic, `prefer_smaller_index`) for indexed and `0` (non-deterministic) for keys, the plain ones to `0`. A `.base` build uses the production selector, a tuning variant forces the backend the file tunes. `TUNE_REQUIREMENT 2` is `prefer_larger_index`. Segment sizes above 8192 are behind `#if 0 // need these` in `common.cuh`, flip it to `#if 1` for the 990-configuration sweep used in sections 2 and 3 (`sed 's|^#if 0 // need these|#if 1 // need these|'`). `cub/benchmarks/bench/topk/` covers `DeviceTopK`. Standard nvbench flags: `--device 0 --timeout 30 --stopping-criterion entropy --throttle-threshold 90 --throttle-recovery-delay 0.15 --skip-time 15e-6`.

### 1.4 Tests

`cub.test.device.segmented_topk_{keys,pairs}.lid_{0,2}.types_{0,1,2}` (twelve binaries, about 724 test cases, include the signed-zero tie-break tests of #11835), `cub.test.device.batched_topk_api`, `cub.test.device.batched_topk_env_api`, `cub.test.device.topk_{keys,pairs,api,env,env_api}`, `cub.test.block.topk`, `cub.test.warp.bitonic_topk`, plus `test_device_batched_topk_requirements_fail.cu` for compile-time rejection.
## 2. Cross-cutting lessons

Each lesson names the implementation it was measured on. Numbers are B200 (CUDA 13.4) unless noted. "The sweep" is the 990-configuration `segmented_topk.variable.indexed.cluster.base` benchmark (float keys, segment sizes 512 to 1M, K 512 to 2048, 1 to 32 segments, five key patterns).

### 2.1 Per-key instructions in the radix passes are the first lever

- One compare plus one select per key per pass, added by the signed-zero fix #11835, cost +6.0% mean and +14% at 1M keys per segment on the sweep (2026-10-08 and again 2026-10-18, `TOPK_RECOUP_INVESTIGATION.md`, `PR11980_description_v2.md`). The dispatch keeps segments resident in shared memory up to several hundred K keys, so every pass re-reads them and every per-key instruction is paid once per pass plus once in the filter. The cost split about evenly between histogram passes and filter, and the kernel is latency-bound, so time tracks executed instructions (post-fix executes 24% more at 1M).
- Spelling matters at the instruction level. The negate twiddle `(bits + m) ^ (m | HIGH_BIT)` with `m = 0 - (bits >> 31)` compiles to a shift, an add and one `LOP3` into which the max-direction complement folds. Variants that call `TwiddleIn` and add or subtract afterwards do not fold and cost one more instruction per key (H200, `PR11835_h200_codegen.md`). Same count of instructions can still cost on B200 when the pipe mix changes (compare and select against shift and `IMAD`), so count and measure.
- A host-side exhaustive check (all 2^32 float patterns, all 16-bit and 8-bit patterns, 2^26 random 64-bit patterns, monotonicity over adjacent values) is cheap and settles ordering questions before any GPU run (`hostcheck` in the recoup study).
- The block top-k carried the same per-key instruction in every path, hidden in a library helper: `ShiftDigitExtractor::Digit` re-applies the -0.0 to +0.0 mapping to every key in every pass although `block_topk_air` already normalizes once in its prologue. A raw shift-and-mask extractor is semantics-preserving and gave fixed-size keys -1.4% and large segments -10% (MSS 4096 -18%) on main, about -1% on the striped layout, at unchanged registers (B200, 2026-10-19, section 4.4). The two implementations place the normalization differently: the cluster agent per key per pass and only for the index tie-breaks (section 3.5), the block top-k once at load time for every path. When passes re-read keys, once at load time is the cheaper placement, and the helpers the passes call need an audit for per-key work that repeats it.

### 2.2 Keep per-key loop bodies straight-line

- ptxas if-converts a short guarded body into predicated instructions, but past a threshold it emits a real branch with `BSSY`, `BRA`, `NOP`, `BSYNC` per key. In the cluster agent's final filter the pre-fix body was predicated, the fix's longer twiddle pushed it over, and the reconvergence bookkeeping then cost more than the twiddle itself at 1M (`TOPK_RECOUP_INVESTIGATION.md`). Static counts of the K=512 / 1M deterministic kernel: pre-fix 9248 instructions and 339 `BSSY`, post-fix 10408 and 415, PR #12060 9544 and 337.
- The fix is to write the guard as data flow: clamp the index into range and force the result with a select (`classify_tile` in #12060). It applies to every tie-break mode, on the non-deterministic filter it removed about 70 compares and 70 selects from the same kernel at an unchanged branch structure.
- ptxas cannot be forced to predicate from the source (the study tried the obvious spellings, see section 3). Design the loop so there is nothing to predicate.

### 2.3 Synchronization and launch floor (cluster agent)

- Every measurement has a 7.7 us floor (kernel duration 5.3 to 6.0 us, SM active 1.6 to 2.2 us), identical for the baseline kernel. Not addressable inside the agent (`CLUSTER_TOPK_B200_PERF_IDEAS.md`, 2026-10-10).
- Cluster barrier lines carry 21 to 42% of stall samples from 64K to 1M keys. Each `barrier.cluster.arrive.release` is a `MEMBAR.ALL.GPU` (about 250 ns), eight barrier pairs per segment. A timing-only probe with relaxed arrives was 8 to 14% faster at every multi-CTA size. This is the strongest unexploited signal.
- Sweeps over resident keys are minor at one segment (0.1 to 3.3 us of 10 to 18 us up to 256K) but 61 of 136 us at 1M with 32 segments, where the filter sweep (43 us) is larger than both later histogram sweeps together (32 us).
- Any per-group decision inside a sweep must cost at most 2 to 3 warp instructions or be amortized (built once per pass). A per-group skip test inside the sweep was +27%, the same idea as a per-pass compacted list was +8% mean and still a loss at 1M (`CLUSTER_TOPK_B200_IDEA_EVALUATIONS.md`).

### 2.4 Shape and tuning facts (cluster agent)

- 768 and 1024 threads per block are 6 to 47% slower everywhere. IPC is 0.7 to 1.6 of 4 with 8 to 16 warps per SM. `no_instruction` stalls are 18 to 29% of samples at one segment (instruction cache, 10,408 SASS instructions per kernel instance), 4% at 32 segments.
- 8-bit digits beat 11-bit by 7 to 12% at 32K to 512K with one segment and lose 6 to 15% where the fourth pass is exposed (1M, or 128K and above with 32 segments). A trade, not a free win. The tuned policy table beats the default 11-bit policy by 6 to 32% at 1K to 128K.
- At 1M only 7 clusters of 16 fit (112 of 148 SMs), 32 segments run in 5 waves. Tie-heavy patterns cost +30 to 45% at 1M (histogram bank conflicts, more candidates).
- The non-deterministic baseline block agent is 25 to 50% faster than a single-CTA cluster launch at 1K to 2K, which is what the 8K crossover encodes.

### 2.5 Block level (block top-k, baseline backend)

- Data arrangement is the biggest lever: striped loads and stores end to end (no exchange barriers) gave fixed-size keys -15 to -17%, pairs -30 to -32%, large keys -33%, and cut registers at MSS 4096 from 127 to 63 (B200, 2026-09-04 and 2026-10-11, `dsb_experiments/exp2/RESULTS_EXP6_FROM_STATUS.md`, `dsb_experiments/prod_check_striped/RESULTS.md`).
- Register tiers decide throughput at 256 threads: 32 registers or fewer give 8 blocks per SM, 40 or fewer give 6. Loop-carried state is where registers hide (recomputing `total_selected` as `k - k_remaining` after the loop took pairs kernels from 80 to 64 registers). Removing work can still lose a tier: skipping the prologue signed-zero normalization moved the 256x4 keys kernels on main from 32 to 38 registers (8 to 6 blocks per SM, +2.5% on fixed-size keys) while the same change on the striped layout was register-neutral and a 1 to 3% win (section 4.4).
- The block-level harness predicts latency well but its register and occupancy effects did not transfer to the device kernels three times in a row (bitmask spelling, exclusive scan, garbage beyond k). Decide occupancy and throughput questions on the device benchmark, read registers with `cuobjdump -res-usage` on the benchmark binary.
- The stock suite stops at MSS 1024 for fixed-size keys, which hid a +9 to +13% regression at MSS 4096 (registers 80 to 103). Keep a large-segment row in every comparison.
- Deterministic index tie-break at block level is affordable: warp-redundant rank with one barrier costs about +1% on random keys and wins +11 to +15% per call under ties against the branch header (`BLOCK_TOPK_DETERMINISTIC_TIE_BREAKING.md`).

### 2.6 Determinism and tie-break routing

- The default contract users get from `DeviceBatchedTopK` with `gpu_to_gpu` plus `prefer_smaller_index` bypasses the block top-k entirely, because only the cluster backend honors it. Any gain in the block path reaches default users only once the block top-k can tie-break (section 4) and the selector routes deterministic requests below 8K to it.
- The deterministic final filter of the cluster agent is the costliest sweep and the one most sensitive to per-key instruction count (sections 2.2 and 3).

### 2.7 Measurement methodology that held up

- Interleave the builds (A, B, C, A, B, C) and run at least two rounds. Quote per-size means and the count of configurations that move by more than 2% in every round, not single configurations. A 990-configuration run takes about 35 s, so rounds are cheap.
- Keep an identical-binary control. On 2026-10-18 the smallest kernels (7 us) jumped by 8 to 9% between consecutive runs of byte-identical kernels while another container loaded the host CPU. Six rounds and per-configuration medians were needed below 8K.
- Compare JSONs with the nvbench revision CCCL pins (`cmake/CCCLGetDependencies.cmake`, `python/scripts/nvbench_compare.py`), which needs Python 3.11 (`enum.StrEnum`) plus `jsondiff tabulate colorama`. On the Mac use a venv and a shim for `StrEnum`.
- SASS: `cuobjdump -sass`, strip addresses and encodings, count instructions and `BSSY` per kernel, pick kernels by the mangled template arguments (`Ll512E`, `Ll1048576E`). A regex that matched addresses of exactly four hex digits (`/\*[0-9a-f]{4}\*/`) truncated every kernel at 4096 instructions. The totals and "zero differing lines" claims in `PR11835_h200_codegen.md`, `PR11835_b200_final_bench.md` and `TOPK_NEGATE_TWIDDLE.md` were produced with it and are unverified, use `{4,}`. A kernel-by-kernel digest comparison (`kdiff.py`) shows exactly which instantiations a change touches.
- Profiling: this nvbench has `--profile` (not `--run-once`), ncu needs `--cap-add=SYS_ADMIN` on the container, per-line stalls come from `ncu --import x.ncu-rep --page source --csv --print-source cuda,sass` on a `-lineinfo` build. Timing-only ablation builds (skip a sweep) apportion phase costs cheaply.
- Trust nothing from a node without a same-session baseline: a fresh main build on the same node and container, run in the same session, is the reference.

### 2.8 Toolchain and infrastructure

- nvcc 12.4 to 12.6 narrows `auto` non-type template arguments of class templates on the host (`cuda::args::constant`), which made `DeviceBatchedTopK` launches fail with error 98. Fixed by normalizing the annotated arguments before naming the kernel (PR #11297, merged 2026-10-12). A plain alias does not fix it (`NVCC_AUTO_NTTP_HOST_DEVICE_DIVERGENCE.md`).
- On the nodes: sccache makes a two-target benchmark build take about 90 s, `git -c commit.gpgsign=false` for rebases and commits (the Mac config has signing on and stalls non-interactively), the pre-commit mypy hook cannot install on the Mac's Python 3.9 (run hooks by id), and `/home/estehle` is an NFS mount that is nearly full.
- dlcluster GB200 nodes (aarch64) have no docker daemon, no cmake and no CUDA toolkit on the bare node. The swarm runs them through an enroot container started per command, with the fork clone and a pip cmake inside, details in section 10.6 and `$SWARM/nodes/README.md`. computelab B200 nodes keep the docker recipe of section 7.

### 2.9 Measured microarchitecture facts for B200 and GB200 (swarm phase 0, 2026-10-25)

Dependent-chain cycles per call in one warp, or in one block of 256 threads where a block is named, chain-slope method of `$SWARM/BRIEF.md` section 4, min of 64 repetitions, CUDA 13.4 Update 2 with nvcc 13.4.92 and `-arch=sm_100` (one row needs `sm_100f`), B200 umbriel-b200-020 and GB200 gb200-nvl4-ts2-92, 2026-10-25. Microbenchmark `$SWARM/harness/micro/intrinsics_micro.cu`, raw JSON and SASS under `$SWARM/state/results/hw-researcher/<node>/`, SASS evidence per row in entries T1 to T19 of `$SWARM/TECHNIQUES.md` (`$SWARM` is defined in section 7). GB200 agrees with B200 within one cycle on every row except the memory fills, so one column serves both and the fills list both (T16). The July 2026 figures of `reference/BLOCK_TOPK_RESULTS.md` section 1 re-verified within a cycle with two exceptions: the uncontended u32 shared atomic with return is 55 cycles on this toolchain, not 115, and the unpacked W2 round is 89 because it now includes the winner select (82 before).

|---|---|---|

| `shfl_xor`, one step | 30.0 | T1 |
| 5-step shuffle max tree, u32, f32, u64 | 150.0, 150.0, 189.8 | T1 |
| `ballot_sync` | 26.7 | T1 |

Rubin (compute capability 10.7, targets `sm_107`, `sm_107f`, `sm_107a`) is covered in `$SWARM/approaches/_hw/RUBIN_NOTES.md` from public documentation only. Nothing is measured, and the CUDA 13.4 release notes call its platform support a developer preview not meant for benchmarking. What matters for this work: Rubin is in the sm_10x family, so an `sm_100f` binary (the one that unlocks `redux.sync.f32`) runs unchanged on B200, GB200, B300 and Rubin. `min` and `max` on `u8x4` and `s8x4` and the packed `set` compare forms on `u8x4`, `s8x4`, `u16x2` and `s16x2` exist only from `sm_107f` (PTX ISA 9.2 and 9.4), so the T8 verdict reverses there and must be re-measured. Shared memory per SM grows to 328 KB, opt-in through the `cudaFuncAttributeSharedMemoryMode` attribute.


Wave 1 additions, measured inside the primitives (B200, nvcc 13.4.92, `-arch=sm_100`, 2026-10-25, harness chain slope, variant and control in one lock section, card files in `$SWARM/cards/`, verification status as in sections 11 to 15):

A dependent redux chain stays at 21 per step up to 16 warps | T33, `harness/micro/intrinsics_micro.cu`, umbriel-b200-091 |
| B2 block round at 256 threads (aggregated post, barrier, read back, head select), IPT 4, 16, 32, 64 | 83.5, 110, 112 to 148, 162 to 205 (bf16), fp32 within 5 | cards `B2_packed_b200_20261025_1` and `_2`, verified |
