Rule distilled from B1: any per-group decision inside a sweep must cost at most 2 to 3 warp instructions or be amortized by building a list once per pass.

### 3.4 Ideas not yet tried, in the order the files rank them

1. A2 (c): after a block barrier one elected thread pays the fence and makes an `mbarrier` arrival, so the arrive no longer costs a GPU-scope fence per thread. First check in SASS whether `MEMBAR.ALL.GPU` survives. Expected 5 to 10%, capped by the 8 to 14% probe. Ranked first because the fence is the largest measured cost not yet exploited.
2. A2 (b): a leader release-store plus a one-thread acquire poll replaces each pass's second barrier. Same reason and range.
3. A3 with C2: after pass 1 compact the candidates into one finishing CTA that breaks ties by an index radix select. Expected 1M from 26.7 to about 17 us and 256K from 18.4 to about 13 us, the only idea with a large gain at every segment count, and the biggest change. It was planned on A1's prefixes, so it now needs the existing prefix exchange (inference in the evaluation file).
4. B1 follow-ups: extend the surviving-group list to the final filter (the largest sweep) and skip list building when the candidate count says nothing is skippable.
5. D2: a register-resident deterministic path for segments up to 8K, expected -20 to -30% at 1K to 4K, building on the faster register block agent (see also section 4.5 for the block-level tie-break that would make this the baseline backend's job).
6. Unordered until measured: D1 code size (5 to 10% at 64K and below, instruction cache), B2 `LDS.128` blocked loads (5 to 10% of sweep time), E1 wave-aware launch shape in the streaming regime (-10 to -20% at 1M with 32 segments), C1 fewer filter polls.
7. Parked: F1 (several clusters per segment through L2, needs A3 first, see section 5), F2 programmatic dependent launch (not measurable with this benchmark), F3 (targets a pipe that is not saturated).

### 3.5 The signed-zero recoup (2026-10-08 to 2026-10-18)

The problem. PR #11835 (merged as 9f71bdb75d) added `NormalizeMinusZero` to the two radix functors in `dispatch_topk.cuh`: `ProcessFloatMinusZero` after `TwiddleIn` maps the twiddled -0.0 (`0x7FFFFFFF`) onto `0x80000000`, one compare and one select per key in every pass and in the filter, only for the two index tie-breaks. Cost on the sweep: +6.02% mean and +14.44% at 1M (B200, 2026-10-08), +5.76% / +5.73% and +13.6% (B200, 2026-10-17), +2.53% and +7.56% (H200, 2026-10-09). The cost splits about evenly between histogram passes and filter, the kernel is latency-bound so time tracks executed instructions, and post-fix executes 24% more of them at 1M (`PR11835_b200_mitigation_experiment.md`, `TOPK_RECOUP_INVESTIGATION.md` ncu section).

The twiddle. Negating sign-set keys instead of complementing them shifts that half up by exactly one, so -0.0 lands on +0.0 and nothing else collides (`TOPK_NEGATE_TWIDDLE.md`, "Why this is safe"). Variants, SASS per key on B200 unless noted, mean and 1M against pre-fix:

| Variant | Per-key code | Result |
|---|---|---|
| `TwiddleIn` plus sign bit (first push) | `TwiddleIn`'s compare and select survive plus `LEA.HI` (H200) | +1.22% mean against the mask form on H200 |
| negate, xor-then-subtract `(bits ^ (m \| H)) - m` (posted on #11980) | shift, two `LOP3`, `IMAD.IADD`: four ops where pre-fix has three | B200 +2.63 to +2.77% mean, +7.4 to +7.8% at 1M. H200 +0.07% |
| negate3, add-then-xor `(bits + m) ^ (m \| H)` | shift, add, one `LOP3` (the complement folds). The later-pass candidate test is six ops against pre-fix's four, because pre-fix's `LOP3` also absorbed the prefix mask | +1.94 / +1.61% mean, +5.1 / +5.3% at 1M |
| fused, fused3: bits prepared once in the first pass, later passes twiddle-free | first pass adds one `STS`, executed instructions -2.4% and -4.2% | fused +0.27 / -0.02%, fused3 +0.05 / -0.24% mean, -1.8 / -1.7% at 1M. About 300 lines of extra plumbing (on the PR #11961 tree), changes the non-deterministic path, an unexplained spread of about 130 configurations more than 2% slower |
| n3f, negate3 plus the branch-free filter (PR #12060) | see 2.2 | +0.31 / +0.14% mean, median 0.00, +1.98 / +2.16% at 1M. Against post-fix -5.23 / -5.29% mean, -10.3% at 1M |
| n3f_skw2, n3f plus a sink bucket and a window compare | kernel 8824 instructions, 225 `BSSY` sites | -2.99 / -2.91% mean, -4.89 / -5.07% at 1M, faster at every size, four configurations consistently over 2% slower (worst +5.2%). All twelve tests pass |

The filter finding is in section 2.2. What was tried to force predication (`TOPK_RECOUP_INVESTIGATION.md` rounds 6 and 7): an unconditional slot computation compiled byte-identical, inline PTX `@p red.shared.add.u32` came back as a branch with the same 391 sites, a stand-alone reproducer branched with both twiddles, three start bits, PTX predication, a hoisted address, one asm block and a vote guard. Only an unconditional sink-bucket atomic avoided the branch, and a later gated filter vote added nine `BSSY` sites again, so the log's advice is to leave the filter alone beyond the clamp.

skw2 (`TOPK_SKW2_WALKTHROUGH.md`, patch `TOPK_n3f_skw2_window_compare_vs_main.patch`, +196 / -36 lines) makes four changes: the add-then-xor twiddle, the clamp, a sink slot `hist[num_buckets + 1]` so later passes always increment and non-candidates hit an unread slot (the replaced branch rarely skipped: with 3% candidates, 62% of warp slots had an entering lane), and a window compare (`init_window`, `is_candidate`, `classify_fast`) that uses the fact that the aligned splitter window never straddles the sign boundary and the twiddle is a translation or reflection within a sign class, so a subtract and a compare on raw bits replace the twiddle and the candidate test drops from five or six ops to three. Floats only. Productization list (walkthrough section 11): integer keys need translation constants and an exhaustive check per width (decomposer keys and `DeviceTopK` are unaffected), the candidate-test form is undecided (three ops leave an unreachable degenerate window, alternatives are four ops or a guard in `init_window`), `init_window` must stay device-side after each fold (device-only factory suggested), remove the EXPERIMENT markers and document the `win_*` members as a struct, examine the register pressure of the seven constants, and run the missing sets (non-deterministic path, H200, a final interleaved B200 set).

Non-deterministic default path of n3f against main (which equals pre-fix there), 2026-10-18: production selector 8K to 1M -1.94 / -1.70 / -1.96% mean over three rounds, -3.4 / -3.2 / -3.2% at 1M, no configuration consistently slower, 198 consistently faster, kernels below 8K byte-identical. With the cluster backend forced, 512 to 4096 is flat over six rounds.

## 4. Block top-k (`block_topk_air`) and the baseline backend

### 4.1 What it is

`detail::block_topk` (`cub/cub/block/block_topk.cuh`) is a facade over `detail::block_topk_air` (`block/specializations/block_topk_air.cuh`). Its only device caller in the library is the per-segment worker of the baseline backend in `agent_batched_topk.cuh`. Keys become order-preserving bits and are processed most significant digit first in 8-bit passes. Each pass builds a shared-memory atomic histogram over the surviving candidates and scans it to find the bucket that holds the k-th key, which becomes the next candidate set without moving data. Partitioning scatters selected items and ties into shared memory through atomic tickets and each thread gathers its slots. The tie subset follows atomic arrival order, so the block top-k is not deterministic today, which is why the selector sends every deterministic or tie-break request to the cluster backend (section 1.1).

### 4.2 Landed: latency work, PR #11060 (merged 2026-09-04)

The August 2026 ablation (`dsb_experiments/BLOCK_TOPK_AIR_ABLATION.md`, `BLOCK_TOPK_AIR_OPT_RESULTS.md`) became PR #11060: fused scan and bucket choice, double-buffered histograms, preset counters, unrolled passes, memoized original keys (`MemoizeKeys`, scatter of the original keys instead of untwiddling), `ShiftDigitExtractor`. Review added an exclusive-scan formulation and dropped the register restore beyond k.

| B200, 2026-08-28 to 2026-09-04 | result | source |
|---|---|---|
| block level, 256 threads x 4 items, k=16, f32 plus i32 pairs | 2583 to 1879 cycles per call, 330 to 404 Gelem/s | `PR_DESCRIPTION_block_topk_air_latency.md` |
| block level, f32 keys only | 2484 to 1822 cycles, 358 to 449 Gelem/s | same |
| `segmented_topk.fixed.keys` vs main | -12.5% mean, best -23.2% (first run), -14.9% mean, median -16.4%, worst -5.0% after the `MemoizeKeys` default flip (bench_air4) | `dsb_experiments/BLOCK_TOPK_AIR_DEVICE_BENCH_RESULTS.md`, memory note |
| `variable.indexed`, `variable.keys` | -2.8% and -2.0% mean, only K equals segment size regresses (worst +4.5%) | same |

What the phase work taught (`BLOCK_TOPK_AIR_OPT_RESULTS.md` sections 1, 2, 6): the critical path is histogram, scan, state broadcast with no independent work, so overlap attempts only move latency onto the next barrier (eager scatter cost 960 cycles, warp specialization 715). Cutting barriers per call from about 16 to about 9 took 26% off the latency. The scan is at its floor at about 377 cycles per pass. Unrolling the passes saved 320 cycles for 16 registers, the largest single win. 8-bit digits beat 10 to 12 bits at 1024 items even where wider digits save a pass. Inline-PTX `BFE` digit extraction blocks address folding (commit 960dd72e9f). With 256 threads, 64-bit same-address shared atomics take 4519 cycles per round, 32-bit fire-and-forget bursts 74.

Review A/Bs worth remembering (`PR11060_pauleonix_review_replies.md`, tables in `dsb_experiments/topk_pr_bench_compare/`): replacing `cuda::bitmask` by shifts is a codegen coin flip (harness +14 registers, device -0.95% mean with pockets both ways), keep `cuda::bitmask`. The exclusive-scan formulation is faster at block level (-2.5 to -4% latency) but moved f32 keys from 40 to 44 registers and cost +2.2% mean, +9.9% worst on `fixed.keys` on the blocked layout, which the landed code carries (inclusive against exclusive at equal switches is within noise once the arrangement is striped, exp6). Redux scan (PR #9840, draft) gives the lowest latency ever measured in the harness but bloats registers, a marginal mixed follow-up.

Small-k side study (`dsb_experiments/BLOCK_TOPK_RESULTS.md`, B200, CUDA 13.1): at 1024 floats and k=16 an iterative shared `atomicMax` extraction beat the stock radix path (2331 against 2499 cycles) and won tie floods, but the optimized radix path later reached 1857 cycles, so it was not productized.

### 4.3 Pending: striped arrangement, `enh/block-topk-striped`

One commit on main 19c1096589 (right after #11060) touching `block_topk_air.cuh`, `block_topk.cuh`, `agent_batched_topk.cuh`, `tuning_batched_topk.cuh` and the block test. The agent loads and stores striped tiles directly, so the shared-memory transposes and their four exchange barriers go. `select_*_striped_to_striped` overloads carry the arrangement as a member-function template parameter, histogram bins are stored striped over threads (`StripedHistogram`, default on, conflict-free scan reads, identity at 256 threads), and `total_selected` is recomputed as `k - k_remaining` after the loop instead of being loop-carried (E2).

| B200, CUDA 13.4.2, 2026-10-11, against unmodified 19c1096589 (`dsb_experiments/prod_check_striped/RESULTS.md`) | mean | best |
|---|---|---|
| `fixed.keys` | -16.9% | -25.7% |
| `fixed.indexed` (pairs throughput, experimental bench) | -32.3% | -56.8% |
| `fixed.keys_large` (MSS 2048 and 4096, experimental) | -33.7% | -50.2% |
| `variable.indexed` | -5.9% | -24.2% |
| `variable.keys` | -3.5% | -15.9% |

No regression survived an A/B/A rerun. The cluster backend is untouched (MSS 8192 and above are identical kernels). Tests and racecheck pass on sm_100 and sm_90. Registers at MSS 4096 drop from 127 to 63 on sm_100. Attribution from exp6 (`dsb_experiments/exp2/RESULTS_EXP6_FROM_STATUS.md`): the striped arrangement alone gives -29.1% on `fixed.indexed` and -16.7% on `fixed.keys`, the striped histogram adds up to -6% on 128-thread tiles only, E2 is within noise except where it moves a register tier (MSS 512 -8.4% on the blocked layout, keys MSS 4096 -22.5%).

State on 2026-10-19: the validated commit is the local 928aee9b3f (worktree `cccl-block-topk-striped`). The fork tip `origin/enh/block-topk-striped` is 56b4cd2832, amended 2026-09-09 with a `tile_exchange_has_storage` static_assert that guards the barrier elision, and it was not re-validated. Upstream main is 514 commits ahead, ten of them touch the branch's files (#11741 variable worker policies, #11673 TempStorage-to-alias simplification, #11199 #11080 #11079 #9801 tunings, #10812 sorted outputs, #11263 #11264 const-correctness, #11995). No PR exists. The barrier elision would race in a tile loop, so any tile-looping or multi-CTA reuse needs the barrier rule from `CUB_EMPTY_TEMP_STORAGE_DESIGN.md`. A 64-bit key caveat from the harness: striped at 256x4 raised f64 keys from 64 to 74 registers (one block per SM less), the device suite is f32 only, so check f64 and u64 device kernels before defaulting it.

It compiles, its tests pass, and it reproduces the gains against that main in the same session (B200, umbriel-b200-018, 2026-10-19: `fixed.keys` -18.0%, `fixed.indexed` -32.3%, `fixed.keys_large` -35.1%, `variable.indexed` and `variable.keys` about -5%, `dsb_experiments/nz_check/RESULTS.md`). It does not carry the fork tip's static_assert, and upstream main is 85 commits past c6a9cbe296 as of 2026-10-24.

### 4.4 Signed-zero normalization and the digit extractor (A/B, 2026-10-19)

Two places handle -0.0 in `block_topk_air`: the prologue maps a twiddled -0.0 onto +0.0 once per key, and `ShiftDigitExtractor::Digit` (through `BaseDigitExtractor::ProcessFloatMinusZero`) repeats that mapping on every key in every pass. The second is dead work once the first has run, and it is also what makes the prologue step impossible to skip on its own: without it, digits and the raw-bit prefix filter and partition disagree, the device assert "Less than k candidates have participated in the histogram" fires and -0.0 outputs come back as zeros from unwritten exchange slots (round 1 of the study, its numbers discarded). Round 2 replaced the extractor by a plain shift and mask (`digit_extractor_t` with `bit_start` and `mask`) and made the prologue step a `NormalizeSignedZero` template parameter, default off, on main c6a9cbe296 (M) and on the striped branch rebased onto it (S). B200, umbriel-b200-018, CUDA 13.4.2, one session, drift canaries 0.00% (`dsb_experiments/nz_check/RESULTS.md`):

| change | `fixed.keys` | `fixed.indexed` (pairs) | `fixed.keys_large` | `variable.indexed` | `variable.keys` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| raw extractor, normalization kept, on main (M to MR) | -1.4% (MSS 1024 -4.3%) | -1.7% | -10.0% (MSS 4096 -18.3%) | +0.3% | -1.3% |
| raw extractor, normalization kept, on striped (S to SR) | -1.3% (MSS 512 -4.5%) | -0.9% | -0.8% | -0.2% | -0.3% |
| normalization skipped on top, main (MR to MNR) | +2.5% (MSS 512 +7.4%) | -2.2% | +6.8% (MSS 4096 +17.7%) | -0.2% | -0.1% |
| normalization skipped on top, striped (SR to SNR) | -0.8% | -0.8% | -2.2% | -0.4% | -0.1% |
| everything against main (M to SNR) | -19.6% | -33.4% | -36.9% | -5.8% | -5.4% |

The raw extractor leaves registers unchanged everywhere and removes 7 to 11% of the SASS instructions in the pass loop. Skipping the prologue normalization is register-neutral on the striped layout, but on main it moves the 256x4 keys kernels from 32 to 38 and 35 registers (8 to 6 blocks per SM), the register lottery of section 2.5 again. Block level (harness, 256x4, k=16, random keys, cycles per call): pairs 1859 to 1836 to 1829 on main and 1865 to 1858 to 1827 on striped, keys 1811 to 1784 to 1778 and 1811 to 1791 to 1779. `cub.test.block.topk` with the +/-0.0 boundary cases, the device segmented top-k keys and pairs tests and the harness contract pass on all four trees. Recommendation: adopt the raw extractor unconditionally (identical semantics, also for a future deterministic path), add the `NormalizeSignedZero` flag only together with it and only on the striped branch, and keep it off for the non-deterministic path. Nothing of this is on a branch yet, the measured headers are `dsb_experiments/nz_check/mr.cuh` (main plus raw extractor) and `snr.cuh` (striped plus raw extractor plus skip).

### 4.5 Deterministic tie-break for the block top-k (study, 2026-10-11)

Only the ties at the k-th key are non-deterministic, and a block-uniform test after the last pass says whether any tie work is needed. Goal: take the `k_remaining` ties with the smallest (or largest) tile index. Seven prototypes on top of 928aee9b3f, B200, CUDA 13.4.2 (`BLOCK_TOPK_DETERMINISTIC_TIE_BREAKING.md` sections 4 to 6, code in `dsb_experiments/det_topk/`). Latency at 256x4 pairs with 3 to 40 ties relative to the branch header:

| approach | latency | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| A, threshold table walked per thread (the fill-index idea) | +18 to +29% | correct, dominated by C, +57% at 256x32 |
| B, warp 0 builds the bases, two barriers | +17 to +22% | 5 to 7 points behind C |
| C, warp-redundant rank: per-warp ballot and popc per item slot into a small table, one barrier, every warp derives bases by a shuffle scan | +11 to +15% | recommended |
| D, block popcount per slot (`__syncthreads_count`) | +16 to +23% | about +50% on large tiles |
| E, radix passes over the index bits | +23 to +44% | worst, grows with ties |
| F, BlockScan in thread order (the cluster agent's method) | +13 to +14% | not index order on striped tiles |
| G, C plus up to 8 ties resolved in the gather | +14 to +21% | better throughput, 4 to 6% tie-free cost |

C wins because it has one barrier and no serial section, keeps only a 32- or 64-bit mask in registers, and its cost does not grow with items per thread. Positions still come from atomic tickets (positions from the rank lost 13 to 22% on random data). Device level against the branch: +0.7% keys and +1.2% pairs on random data, +10.9% and +12.9% with heavy duplicates (entropy 0.201), -15.4% and -22.4% with all keys equal. The +11% at MSS 512 in the prototype is a register artifact (32 to 36 registers on the 256x4 partial-tile kernel). A compile-time specialization of the partition loop for the select-all case was 21% faster at K=512 on the device but cost 7% per call at k=16 in the harness, to be landed separately in a single-loop form. No branch productizes C yet. Productization path (`BLOCK_TOPK_DETERMINISTIC_WALKTHROUGH.md` section 5): tie-break template parameter on `block_topk_air`, facade and agent carry `Determinism` and `TieBreak` like the cluster agent, `policy_selector_from_types` routes deterministic requests below the 8192 crossover to the baseline, watch registers per instantiation, blocked overloads use BlockScan (index order there).

### 4.6 Device-side benchmarking

`dsb_experiments/DEVICE_SIDE_BENCHMARKING_ISSUE.md` documents the gaps of `nvbench_helper/device_side_benchmark.cuh` (throughput scales with occupancy, the latency mode lets calls overlap and underestimates by about a third at length 256, nothing reports resources) and proposes latency, throughput and resource regimes plus a block top-k benchmark. Nothing of it is upstream. The workaround that produced every block-level number above: standalone harnesses where latency is the slope of `clock64` cycles over chain lengths in one block with a data-dependent link and a SASS check, and throughput is a fixed element-normalized workload (`dsb_experiments/exp2/proto_air_integration.cu` against the real header, `det_topk/det_harness.cu` for ties and striped tiles, `proto_latency.cu` and `proto_throughput.cu` as the method demos). Device level is the segmented top-k nvbench suite with base and branch built in one session, A/B/A reruns and the baseline repeated last (`prod_check_striped/launch_prod_check.sh <node> <container> <arch> <label>`, `det_topk/det_device.sh`). `run_remote.sh` hardcodes an August node and container, check both before use. Registers per device kernel: `cuobjdump -res-usage` on the benchmark binary, template order in the mangled name is (K, MSS).

## 5. Multi-CTA batched top-k (planned)

No code exists yet. This section collects what the cluster and block studies say about a design where several CTAs cooperate on one segment without a thread-block cluster. Everything here is inference from measured facts in sections 2 to 4 unless a file is named.

### 5.1 What the numbers say about the design space

- The cluster agent's time at large segments is dominated by two things: the sweeps over resident keys (per-key instructions, section 2.1 and 2.2) and the cluster barriers (eight `MEMBAR.ALL.GPU` pairs per segment, 21 to 42% of stalls, section 2.3). A multi-CTA design replaces distributed-shared-memory reductions and cluster barriers with global-memory reductions and some form of inter-CTA synchronization. Each such synchronization is at least a GPU-scope release and acquire, which is exactly the cost the relaxed-arrive probe removed (about 250 ns per fence, 8 to 14% end to end). The design question is therefore the number of global synchronization points per segment, not their individual cost. Fewer passes (wider digits where the pass count drops, or a candidate-compaction finish as in idea A3 of `CLUSTER_TOPK_B200_PERF_IDEAS.md`) is the lever, see section 3.
- Per-key work transfers unchanged: the negate twiddle, the branch-free filter and the straight-line loop discipline apply to any radix pass over keys in shared memory or registers. Reuse `extract_bin_op_t` and `identify_candidates_op_t` from `dispatch_topk.cuh` rather than new functors, so the signed-zero semantics stay identical across backends.
- Residency is the other transferable idea: the cluster agent keeps each CTA's chunk resident across passes and only streams the overflow. A multi-CTA design that re-reads global memory every pass pays HBM bandwidth per pass, so plan the resident set per CTA (registers or shared memory) first and size the CTA count from it.
- The baseline block agent is 25 to 50% faster than a single-CTA cluster launch at 1K to 2K keys and the production crossover sits at 8K (section 2.4). A multi-CTA backend competes above 8K, where the cluster backend currently wins, and should be measured against the cluster backend with the sweep's 8K to 1M rows and against the baseline below 8K only as a sanity check.
- Wave quantization matters at the top of the range: with clusters of 16 CTAs only 112 of 148 SMs are busy at 1M (section 2.4). CTAs that are not tied into clusters can fill every SM, which is one concrete argument for the multi-CTA design at the largest segments, and it is measurable before any code exists by comparing the cluster agent's 1M rows at 1 and 32 segments.
- Determinism costs are known at both ends: the deterministic final filter with index tie-break is the largest sweep in the cluster agent, and at block level a warp-redundant rank adds about 1% on random keys (section 2.5). A multi-CTA finish could do the tie-break in one CTA over compacted candidates (idea A3), which the cluster evaluation ranked high and never built. The straddling-CTA tie-break carries over if CTAs own contiguous index ranges (`PR9224_code_review.md` section 6.4 argues there is at most one straddler).
- Scaffolding already exists. The baseline arm of `dispatch_batched_topk.cuh` carries an unreachable multi-worker-per-segment path: the baseline agent (`agent_batched_topk_worker_per_segment`) enqueues every segment larger than one tile into a large-segments list with an atomic counter, segment ids and per-segment tile counts, the dispatch runs a transform scan over tile counts into `large_segment_tile_offset_t` offsets, the epilogue reads those offsets, and `multi_worker_policy` plus the baseline `epilogue` sub-policy exist but are zero-initialized. A `TODO(topk)` at the allocation code names what is missing: overflow guards on the byte counts, populated sub-policies with benchmark knobs, and the worker itself. Start there rather than from a blank file.
- Host overhead is part of the budget: the cluster launch path probes occupancy with up to about 35 runtime calls per launch and no memoization (`PR9224_code_review.md` section 5.3, loop at `dispatch_batched_topk.cuh` 422 to 452 at fc8f07f951). A multi-CTA launch without clusters avoids that probe entirely.
- What does not transfer: DSMEM atomics, `mapa`, hardware cluster barriers and remote `mbarrier` arrivals. What the A1 result says about the replacement: posted atomics into one histogram beat every CTA gathering every peer's histogram, since gather cost grows with the CTA count. Global memory offers no cheaper fence than the cluster arrive already pays, and its fences also wait on L2 atomics. Keeping keys resident across passes needs a persistent kernel with a grid barrier, a kernel per pass re-reads the segment each time.

### 5.2 Questions to settle before building

1. Synchronization primitive and count per segment: cooperative launch with grid sync, a decoupled look-back style counter per pass, or a split into one kernel per pass as `DeviceTopK` does since PR #8299. Count the GPU-scope fences per segment for each option on paper first.
2. Digit width and pass count for 32-bit and 64-bit keys, with the 8-bit against 11-bit trade from section 2.4 in mind (the fourth pass is what hurts at 1M).
3. Residency: registers against shared memory per CTA, the CTA count per segment as a function of segment size, and whether the overflow stream of the cluster agent is needed at all when CTAs are not limited to a cluster.
4. Tie-break and determinism placement: inside every CTA's filter (as the cluster agent does) or once in a finishing CTA over compacted candidates.
5. Selector integration: where the new backend sits between the 8K crossover and the cluster backend, and how `policy_selector_from_types` chooses among three backends.

### 5.3 What to measure first

- Reproduce the cluster agent's sweep on the target node with pre-fix, post-fix and PR #12060 to get a same-session reference (scripts in `dsb_experiments/topk_recoup_b200_20261017/node3/in/`).
- A timing-only probe of the chosen synchronization primitive at the planned count per segment, in the style of the relaxed-arrive fence probe, before writing any selection logic.
- Phase attribution of the cluster agent at 8K to 1M from `dsb_experiments/cluster_topk_perf/phase_attribution.txt`, to know which phase the new design must beat.

## 9. Conventions for this document

- This markdown file is the source of truth. A private rendered copy is regenerated from it, never edited by hand: `python3 dsb_experiments/topk_kb/build_page.py TOPK_KNOWLEDGE_BASE.md /tmp/topk-knowledge-base.html`, then publish that file with the Artifact tool with `url` set to the link (a publish without `url` creates a separate artifact). The artifact is private to the owner until shared from the page's Share menu.
- How a session finds this document: the maintainer's sessions in the main checkout get it from the memory index (`topk-knowledge-base.md`). Any other session is pointed at it with one line in the prompt, for example `Read the top-k knowledge base first`, naming this branch copy or the master document, or with that sentence in the user's Claude Code instructions file or in a `CLAUDE.local.md` inside a checkout (gitignored). A session without access to the maintainer's machine reads this branch copy.
- Every number carries hardware, CUDA version, benchmark name and date, and a file where the full table lives. Means over the 990-configuration segmented benchmark are not comparable to means over the block-level harness, say which one.
- Append a dated entry to the ledger (section 8) when a study ends, and update the status board (section 6) when a branch, PR or validation changes. Newest first in the ledger.
- Keep the body short and point to the reports for detail. A new report goes into the repo root (or `dsb_experiments/`) and gets one line in section 7.
- A new algorithmic approach (a new backend, a new block or warp primitive) gets its own top-level section with the skeleton of sections 3 and 4: what it is, where the time goes, ideas tried with verdicts, ideas not yet tried, open items, pointers. Its numbers are reported on the benchmark set of section 1.3 against a same-session main build on the same node, with the segment-size regime stated (below 8K keys the baseline block backend serves, 8K to 1M the cluster backend), so that approaches compare cell by cell and not each on its own benchmark. A lesson that holds beyond one approach moves to section 2 with the implementation it was measured on named.
- No em dashes and no semicolons in prose (the user's convention for anything that may be pasted into a PR).
- Section numbering is stable. Sections 6 (status board), 7 (where things live), 8 (ledger) and 9 (conventions) keep their numbers because `$SWARM/BRIEF.md` and the swarm agents cite them. The swarm study is section 10 and its per-approach sections are appended after it as they open, each with the skeleton above.
- Swarm primitives (section 10) report their numbers on the harness cells of `$SWARM/harness/cells_focus.json` (cycles per call in the latency regime, Gelem/s with occupancy and registers in the throughput regime) against the control named in the card, measured in the same lock section, with arch, CUDA version, node, date, commit and the card file. Device-level numbers for approaches wired into a batched agent use the section 1.3 benchmark set as before. A number never compares two archs.

- Harness versions. Ruling of the lead (2026-10-25 19:40 UTC, now in `cards/README.md`): a card is judged under the harness version it was measured with, because its variant and control shared one lock section and one harness build. When a later harness changes a control's net number, the verifier records the current-version ratios in the card next to the card's own and the card stays verified if the conclusions hold under both. Comparisons across cards measured under different harness versions are not made, and the final evaluation re-measures every winner under one version in one session. This document quotes a card's own numbers as its record and the verifier's current-version ratios next to them where they differ (section 21 is the first case), and never ranks two cards measured under different harness versions against each other in section 16 without saying so.

- Verdict words. For an approach or a variant: passes (it met the bar of its idea card), partial (it met part of the bar, or it survives for a sub-band, as a finish or as a fallback), killed (it missed the bar and gets no further GPU time). For a card: verified (a verifier rebuilt it from its commit and reproduced it within tolerance), refuted (the numbers or the contract do not reproduce), superseded (replaced by a later card named in `superseded_by`). The phase 0 cost-model rejections of 10.5 (R1 to R11, never measured) keep the word rejected, and older sentences that say retired or closed mean killed.

## 10. Top-k cooperative primitives: the swarm study (started 2026-10-25)

### 10.1 What it is

A multi-agent study, run from this Mac in Claude Code, to find the fastest sub-warp, warp and block level top-k building blocks for key-index pairs and to say, per cell of key type, scope, N, k, requirement and regime, which approach wins and what each approach's determinism and ordering support costs. Deliverables: a winner matrix (`WINNERS.md`), this knowledge base grown with one top-level section per approach, the techniques catalogue, an issue tree document (`GITHUB_ISSUE_TREE.md`, the swarm creates no issues), and the prototypes, harness and node scripts on the fork branch `exp/topk-primitives-swarm` under `dsb_experiments/topk_swarm/`, called `$SWARM` here and listed in section 7. Plan and decisions: `$SWARM/PLAN.md` sections 3, 4, 9 and 12. The binding contract for every agent: `$SWARM/BRIEF.md`.

Focus set, for optimizer iteration and the first evaluation: bf16 and fp32 keys, int32 indices, output indices only (keys are never returned), requirements `not_guaranteed` unsorted and `prefer_smaller_index` unsorted, random input, both regimes, k on the coarse grid 4, 16, 64, 256, 1024, 4096 bounded by N over 2, scopes sub-warp (logical warps 4, 8, 16 with N 8 to 128), warp (N 32 to 4096) and block (256 threads, N 1024 to 32768), in `$SWARM/harness/cells_focus.json`. The full evaluation adds fp8 e4m3, fp16, fp64 and pair output, sorted output is measured once at the end. Methodology (`BRIEF.md` section 4): latency as the chain-length slope of `clock64` cycles per call with a generate-only control subtracted, throughput as one occupancy wave in Gelem/s with occupancy and registers reported, same-session A/B of variant and control inside one GPU lock section, a host oracle before any number, default build `-arch=sm_100` with an optional `sm_100f` variant reported separately. Radix and atomics approaches twiddle internally, comparison approaches compare the floating type natively, minus zero equals plus zero under the tie-break requirement, focus-set inputs contain no NaN.

Roles: the lead (the main session), the broker (nodes), the algorithm researcher (idea cards, does not code), the hardware researcher (techniques catalogue), the evaluation engineer (harness, oracle, final evaluation), one optimizer per approach per wave, one verifier per result card, and the curator (this document). Phases: 0 setup and 1 wave 1 ended on 2026-10-25 (sections 11 to 16), wave 2a launched at 14:45 UTC the same day as uniquely named agents in their own worktrees (opt-B4 with N9, opt-W5 with N7, opt-N6w with W3 and N1, opt-N8, exp-W1codegen), wave 2b started on 2026-10-25 15:35 UTC with opt-N5 and opt-N10 (B6, N3 and W4 wait for B4), then 3 final evaluation on one node in one session, 3b sorted output, 4 reporting. Wave 1 ran as a workflow whose retries resumed agent ids, which produced duplicate optimizer instances for W1 and B2, resolved by splitting their slices (W1 and W1b, B2 instances A and B). Wave 2 uses named agents instead. State at 18:05 UTC the same day: opt-N8 (16:00, two-node repeat 16:50), opt-W5 (16:30) and opt-N10 (17:00) have finished and are sections 17 to 19, all their cards verified by 17:50 UTC except `W5_v3` (refuted on contract). umbriel-b200-020 is retired as a slow outlier (T58, 2.10). opt-N5 (section 20, verified at 18:25 UTC) and opt-B4 with N9 (section 21, verify-B4 running on two nodes) finished at 18:05 UTC, opt-B6 is killed (R17), opt-N6w finished at 18:35 UTC on GB200 (section 22, verify-N6w rerunning on B200), opt-W4 finished at 19:00 UTC (section 23, verify-W4 running), opt-B1det at 18:50 UTC (13.6, verify-B1det running), opt-N3 (one cell against B4) and opt-B1det3 (13.7) are running, and harness 0.10 (2.10) is the version of the final evaluation, frozen. Phase 3 started at 19:55 UTC: plan `$SWARM/harness/PHASE3_PLAN.md` and adapter manifest `harness/phase3_adapters.json` (25 adapter rows across block, warp and sub-warp with `B1_reference`, `W1_reference` and `W1_baseline_sub` as the controls in the same lock section, `B1_det` as a second control for the deterministic cells), cells the focus grid plus the flip bands plus the named workloads, the random pass first and tie_heavy on cells within 15 percent, one dedicated B200 other than 020 with a hardware pre-flight, then a GB200 pass and phase 3b sorted output, outputs `WINNERS_b200.md` and `WINNERS_gb200.md` from `tools/winners.py` with the margin over the runner-up and the reference, node and harness version per cell. The block pass completed at 21:15 UTC and the block part of section 16 is rebuilt from `WINNERS_b200.md` (446 cells, 144 close calls that stay marked), the warp and sub-warp pass runs next, then GB200 and phase 3b.

### 10.2 Workloads the cells stand for (checked against model configs 2026-10-25)

Figures from `$SWARM/ideas/WORKLOADS.md` (Hugging Face configs, papers and vendor docs, every number cited inline there, machine-readable in `ideas/named_cells.json`). This table supersedes `PLAN.md` section 3.1 where they differ.

| Workload | N per call | k | Scope | Where in WORKLOADS.md |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MoE token-choice routing: Mixtral 8 top 2, DBRX 16 top 4, gpt-oss 32 and 128 top 4, Qwen3 128 top 8, GLM-4.5 160 top 8, DeepSeek-V3 and R1 256 top 8, DeepSeek-V4-Flash 256 top 6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro 384 top 6, Kimi K2 384 top 8, Qwen3-Next and Qwen3.5 512 top 10, Nemotron 3 Ultra 512 top 22, LongCat 768 top 12, Kimi K3 896 top 16, more in the file. Llama 4 is top 1 and out of scope. Scores are usually fp32 after sigmoid or softmax, bf16 when the gate output is taken directly | 8 to 896 | 1 to 22 | sub-warp for N at most 64, warp with 8 to 32 items per lane for the 2026 flagships | section 1 |
| Grouped routing (DeepSeek V3 lineage and Ling-1T only): top 2 of 32 per group, top 4 of 8 groups, top 8 of the remaining 128 | 8 to 128 | 2 to 8 | sub-warp and warp, three ordinary cells | section 1, R11 |
| PEER product keys: top 16 over each of two 1024 sub-key sets, top 16 of 256 sums, 8 heads per token | 256 to 1024 | 16 | warp | section 2 |
| Expert choice and mixture-of-depths: k over N is c over E, 1 over 64 to 1 over 8 | 1024 to 8192 | N over 64 to N over 8 | block | section 3 |
| Sampling and top-p over the vocabulary (128K to 262K today, 32K is legacy), k 20 to 50 by default, chunked per block then merged | 4096 to 32768 per chunk | 32 to 128 | block, merge at warp or block | sections 4 and 7 |
| Beam search (4 to 8 beams over 512K to 2M scores) and EAGLE-2 draft trees (top 10 per node, top 60 rerank over about 600, children top 10 of 100) | 32768 per chunk, rerank 1024, children 128 | 8 to 64 | block and warp | section 4 |
| DSA sparse attention indexer: V3.2 k 2048, V4 Pro 1024, V4 Flash 512 over 32K to 256K fp32 scores, a deterministic production variant exists | 32768 per chunk, merge 4096 to 8192 | 512 to 2048 | block | sections 5 and 7 |
| NSA: N is the context over 64, k 16 (13 chosen), fp32 | 512 to 2048 | 16 | warp | section 5 |
| MoBA: top 3 of 16 to 64 blocks, top 12 of 256 | 16 to 256 | 3 to 12 | sub-warp and warp | section 5 |
| Quest page selection (pages of 16, budget 256 to 4096) | 2048 to 8192 | 16 to 256 | block | section 6 |
| SnapKV (budget 1024 to 4096 on 4K to 32K prompts) and H2O (20 percent of the cache) | 2048 to 32768 | N over 32 to N over 4 | block | section 6 |

The five corrections to the plan (`WORKLOADS.md` section 9): routing is N 8 to 896 and k 1 to 22, not N up to 384 and k up to 8, and the 2026 flagships are warp cells with 8 to 32 items per lane. Capacity routing is a selection regime (k over N 1 over 64 to 1 over 8), the large k over N regime comes from chunk merges (1 over M for M chunks), H2O and SnapKV. DSA k is 512, 1024 or 2048 over 32K to 256K fp32 entries with a deterministic variant. Vocabularies are 128K to 262K, sampling k 20 to 50, draft trees top 10 per node with a top 60 rerank, SGLang defaults to top 4. bf16 inputs must come from a logit-like distribution with the tie density at rank k reported.

bf16 ties by construction (`WORKLOADS.md` section 8). bf16 keeps 7 mantissa bits, so each binade holds 128 representable values, and router logits or attention scores span two to four binades. A row of 256 to 896 bf16 keys draws from a few hundred distinct values and ties at the k-th key are the norm. Consequences: the harness generates every pattern in fp32 (random is unit normal) and rounds to the key type, and from the batch after the phase 0 references on each cell carries `ties_at_k_mean` and `ties_at_k_max` over its 16 inputs. The gap between `not_guaranteed` and `prefer_smaller_index` is material for bf16, radix and bit-serial approaches cannot count on early exit for bf16, and the packed key-plus-inverted-index word (W2, B2, N4) resolves ties by index at zero cost. fp32 sigmoid or softmax scores are not tie-heavy but cluster within a few binades, which matters for N4's truncated keys and for radix pass 1.

Chunk merges (`WORKLOADS.md` section 7). Every vocabulary, DSA, beam and draft-tree workload exceeds one block and decomposes into per-chunk top-k (block N 32768 or 4096) plus one merge over M k candidates with k over N equal to 1 over M, the regime where B1's epilogue, B6's sort and a k-truncated multiway merge of sorted runs (W4, B6) compete.

### 10.4 Harness and the phase 0 reference batches

Harness contract: `$SWARM/harness/README.md` (adapter interface, three input arrangements, an oracle battery of nine patterns plus the cell's own, latency and throughput drivers, canary, resources, JSON). Two reference adapters exist, `B1_reference` (`block_topk_air` through the pairs path, `not_guaranteed` only) and `W1_reference` (`WarpBitonicTopK`, MaxK rounded up to 32, `not_guaranteed` only), so every `prefer_smaller_index` cell is unsupported until an adapter supports it.

Reference cards, 2026-10-25: `$SWARM/cards/B1_reference_b200_20261025_1.json`, `B1_reference_gb200_20261025_1.json`, `W1_reference_b200_20261025_1.json` and `W1_reference_gb200_20261025_1.json`. B200 umbriel-b200-068 and GB200 gb200-nvl4-ts2-100, CUDA 13.4.92, `-arch=sm_100`, commit fc5de9f42987, random input (bf16 rounded from the unit normal, so bf16 rows tie at rank k, 1.4 to 54 keys equal to the k-th in the B1 cells), requirement `not_guaranteed`. All oracles passed (546 cases per B1 card, 494 per W1 card), A/A controls (the adapter is its own control in one lock section), canaries flat at 32.0 cycles per op at start and end, 84 cells per B1 card and 76 per W1 card, not yet verified by a verifier. The full tables with controls, raw and no-op slopes, fit quality, harness ceiling, occupancy, registers, spills and tie multiplicity are in `$SWARM/approaches/B1/REFERENCE.md`, `approaches/W1/REFERENCE.md` and `RESULTS_BOARD.md`. Latency in cycles per call net of the control, throughput in Gelem/s, the two archs side by side as the consistency check the cards make themselves, not as a comparison:

| Cell | B200 | GB200 |
|---|---|---|
| B1, block of 256 threads, bf16, N 1024, k 16 | 1882 cycles, 350 Gelem/s (40 registers in the latency kernel, 32 and 8 blocks per SM in the throughput kernel) | 1882 cycles, 375 Gelem/s |
| B1, fp32, N 1024, k 16 | 1905 cycles, 355 Gelem/s (44 registers, 38 and 6 blocks per SM) | 1905 cycles, 382 Gelem/s |
| B1, bf16, N 16384, k 16 | 9679 cycles, 526 Gelem/s (122 registers, 2 blocks per SM) | 9653 cycles, 567 Gelem/s |
| B1, fp32, N 16384, k 16 | 10463 cycles, 310 Gelem/s (169 registers, 1 block per SM) | 10424 cycles, 333 Gelem/s |
| W1, warp, fp32, N 128, k 16 (k 4 and k 16 pay the MaxK 32 network) | 2041 cycles, 179 Gelem/s (32 registers) | 2038 cycles, 192 Gelem/s (33 registers) |
| W1, bf16, N 128, k 16 | 2267 cycles, 106 Gelem/s (36 registers) | 2267 cycles, 114 Gelem/s |
| W1, fp32, N 512, k 16 | 9970 cycles, 171 Gelem/s (56 registers) | 10281 cycles, 183 Gelem/s (63 registers) |
| W1, bf16, N 512, k 16 | 12606 cycles, 97 Gelem/s (63 registers) | 12586 cycles, 104 Gelem/s |

What the cards establish (their findings fields):

- Key width. bf16 is not cheaper than fp32 for the AIR block primitive in latency (1882 against 1905 at N 1024 k 16, 9679 against 10463 at N 16384 k 16): two 8-bit passes with no early exit cost about what fp32's two to four passes with early exit cost, and bf16 wins throughput only where it buys occupancy (N 16384: 526 against 310 Gelem/s at 2 against 1 blocks per SM). In the warp bitonic reference bf16 is slower than fp32 in every cell (778 against 583 cycles at N 32, 2267 against 2041 at N 128, 12606 against 9970 at N 512, 76k against 49k at N 2048, 156k against 130k at N 4096): 16-bit keys travel through 32-bit shuffle lanes with pack and unpack and are compared with `__hgt`, 24 to 57 percent more SASS instructions for the same network. A 16-bit win needs two keys per lane (W1 card, N5), not the scalar compare.
- Arch. GB200 matches B200 in cycles within 1 percent on every B1 cell and every bf16 W1 cell, throughput is 7 to 10 percent higher on GB200 from clocks (the fixed FMA canary runs in 3614 against 3778 us). The W1 fp32 cells are the exception: up to 4 percent slower on GB200 from the same commit and nvcc version with a different register count (19 against 22 at N 32, 56 against 63 at N 512), and the GB200 A/A control spreads by 2 to 4 percent at N 2048. Resolved on 2026-10-25 by the evaluation engineer: the aarch64 build of cicc emits branches around the compare-exchange swaps where the x86 build emits selects, ptxas re-predicates both but leaves 16 more instructions and 3 more registers (T20, 2.10), a toolchain artifact and not a hardware effect. The reference cards remain unverified by a verifier but served as the same-section control of 20 verified cards.
- B1 shape. Latency grows slowly with k at fixed N (fp32 N 1024: 1785 at k 4 to 2313 at k 256) and about linearly with N at fixed small k (fp32 k 16: 1905, 3705, 10463, 18955 for N 1024 to 32768), the histogram sweep dominating above N 4096 at about 85 cycles per item per pass as the B1 card's model says. Registers scale with items per thread (44 at IPT 4 fp32, 64 at 16, 169 at 64, 255 with about 1 KB of spill at 128), so throughput peaks at N 4096 (521 to 574 Gelem/s at 4 blocks per SM) and falls to 310 to 376 at N 16384 and 32768. Index-only output is not cheaper than pairs output here, because `block_topk_air` has no values-only epilogue and the adapter runs the pairs exchange and drops the keys, the first saving for the B1 optimizer to measure.
- W1 shape. k 4 and k 16 cost the k 32 network, latency is linear in N for fixed MaxK (fp32 k 16: 2041, 9970, 49343 for N 128, 512, 2048) until registers run out at N 4096 (255 registers with spills, 2.6 times the N 2048 cost), 18 to 32 cycles per input element, sorted and deterministic output for free, an order of magnitude more per element than B1 at matching N. The buffered variant of PR #9281 for N much larger than MaxK is the open question.
- Continuity with July 2026. Main's `block_topk_air` measures 1905 cycles at N 1024 k 16 fp32 against the July v6 prototype's 1857 (section 4.2), and the warp network 2041 at N 128 k 16 against the July 2500 once the July chain and staging overheads are removed. The harness pitfalls found on the way are in 2.10. Re-measurement under harness 0.6 (2026-10-25 17:05 UTC, commit 93b8ff594c): the B200 B1 reference card was replaced by a run on umbriel-b200-020 (commit bb116b9775, batch `state/results/eval-engineer/b200/B1_reference_vs_B1_reference_focus_focus3_20261025_154557.json`, 84 cells, A/A), so the B200 column above is the phase 0 record from umbriel-b200-068 and the card now reads 1892 (bf16) and 1891 (fp32) cycles at N 1024 k 16 (350 and 356 Gelem/s), 10018 and 10731 at N 16384 k 16 (527 and 310 Gelem/s). The large-N change is the chain-16 fit fix of 2.10, not the node. The W1 reference cards and the GB200 B1 card were not re-measured. Re-measured again under harness 0.8 on umbriel-b200-068 after the 020 retirement (commit 40ccd445ae, batch `..._focus4_20261025_161755.json`, the card now names 068): 1892 and 1891 at N 1024 k 16, 9609 and 10391 at N 16384 k 16, throughput identical to the 020 run, latency within 3 percent in 32 of 42 cells and 3 to 5 percent lower in ten large-N cells. The GB200 reference is within 3 percent of it in latency everywhere and 6 to 7 percent higher in throughput.

### 10.5 Negative results ledger

One line per idea that did not pay, with the reason and the file. The phase 0 entries are rejections on the cost model by the algorithm researcher, not measurements. Measured negatives from result cards join this list with their numbers and design points.

| Id | Idea | Why | Label |
|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Tournament trees over per-thread sorted runs | A full redux max (22) is cheaper than a path-only update through shuffles (5-step tree 150), and at block scope the flat fire-and-forget atomic round (74) beats the hierarchical form (148) because the MIO combiner already is the tree. B2 and W2 are the tournament tree with the hardware as the tree | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R2 | Bucket select with a wide first digit (16-bit for bf16) | The scan sits on the critical path of every pass and grows superlinearly with the bin count (10-, 11-, 12-bit digits measured 2063, 2404, 3342 cycles against 1779 for 8-bit at N 1024 in the August AIR sweep), 65536 bins need 256 bins per thread in the scan and 64 KB of counters. For fp8 one 8-bit pass is simply B1. The goal survives as N6 | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R3 | k-ary search and sample-sort multi-pivot selection as separate approaches | Per-round per-item cost grows with the pivot count exactly as the shrink factor does, the one measured k-ary result (merge-path probes) was negative at every size, and the first pivot from a sorted sample is W5's and B4's bound. Folded into W5 and B4 | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R4 | Speculative sub-digit histogram (4096 counters in pass 1) | The histogram phase is MIO-issue bound, so two atomics per item double pass 1 (about 340 more cycles at N 1024, about 11000 at N 32768) to save a pass 2 sweep that costs the same at N 1024 and far less at N 32768, where it is predicated to about N over 256 candidates | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R5 | Count-based early termination in radix passes | Already present: `block_topk_air` exits when the bucket count equals k remaining and the small-candidate finish is the measured FINISH knob, and nothing in a histogram pass can stop before all items are counted. Survives as B1 explore items 5 and 6 | rejected as already present, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R6 | `match.any` pre-aggregation of histogram atomics | The same-address burst is already combined by the MIO and pre-reducing with redux made rounds slower (148 against 74). The revival threshold was a `match.any` under 10 cycles, T5 measured 51 at one distinct value and 392 at 32 | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, the instruction cost it hinged on was measured (T5), the kernel was not |
| R7 | An expert-choice specific primitive | The per-expert selection is an ordinary top-k column with k over N equal to c over E (1 over 64 to 1 over 8), nothing is shared between experts except N and k, and the token-major layout is the device agent's transpose problem. Survives as the `ec_*` and `mod_*` named cells | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R8 | Threshold reuse across rows in the batched setting | Across independent rows the previous threshold is a guess that needs verification, a retry ladder and a fallback, and a stale guess cost GVR V1 3.6 to 6x. Within one row the prefix threshold is an exact bound, which is PR #9281's buffered algorithm and N3 | rejected across rows on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured, accepted within a row as N3 |
| R9 | Exact rank by 31 pairwise shuffle compares for N at most 32 | 31 dependent shuffles at about 35 cycles is about 1100 cycles against N1 at about 640, W2 at 656 (k 8) and a sort32 at 660 | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R10 | Block histogram built by per-warp ballots instead of shared atomics | An 8-bit digit needs 8 ballots per item and 256 bucket counts, about 64 instructions per lane per item at 4 items, above 500 cycles per pass against the 340-cycle atomic histogram, and 4-bit digits double the passes. The warp-redundant read of a small shared table (4.5 approach C) stays available for tie counts | rejected on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R11 | A fused grouped-routing selector as a CUB primitive | A composition of three ordinary cells with a layout contract, not a primitive, and the 2026 flagships (DeepSeek V4, Kimi K2 and K3, GLM-4.5, Qwen3.5, Nemotron 3) have one group. Survives as the decomposition in 10.2 and the `deepseek_v3_*` named cells | rejected as a primitive on the cost model, 2026-10-25, not measured |
| R12 | N2, level-parallel bitonic top-k (sort all tiles at once, halve per level) | With identical network code and policies the restructuring gains nothing: kill cell N 64 k 32 0.96x bf16 and 1.00x fp32, N 128 to 256 0.95x to 1.08x at warp scope, 0.91x to 1.17x at sub-warp scope, pairs 1.09x to 1.22x with 12 to 34 more registers and lower throughput. ptxas already overlaps the eager loop's independent tile sorts. A differently written level network (opt-W1, VIMNMX exchange, template recursion) beat the CUB network by 1.5x to 2.5x on GB200, attributed on 2026-10-25 by exp-W1codegen (11.4): the level order itself is worth 1.15x to 1.54x only with one word per tile in latency and nothing in throughput, the rest of that gap is the min max exchange (T42) and the code structure, so R12 stays killed as a general code path and is reopened for the one-word-per-tile latency cells with N at least 4 MaxK only | rejected on measurement, 2026-10-25, cards `W1_level_same_b200_20261025_1`, `W1_level_same_sub`, `W1_level_pairs` (B200 umbriel-b200-020, verified by verify-W1b to the cycle) |

B4 and N9 (verified): per-slot uniform branches in the finish (T56), branch-free tickets with return to dummy words (170 to 270 cycles worse), B4 below N 8192 (0.90x to 0.95x at N 4096, 0.74x at N 1024) and at k 2048 (0.57x to 0.81x), N9 at its k 16 kill cells (plus 2 to 3 percent), the constant-address ticket (T57). N6w, W3, N1 (verified): chunked ballot issue (v2, a no-op, 15 to 30 percent slower at bf16 N 2048), the trimmed finish (atom32f, 2 to 8 percent slower at IPT 8 to 32), N6w at k 16 (2.4x to 4.8x behind W2) and at N 4096 (255 registers, a 4 KB stack, 60 to 80 thousand cycles), W3 as a primitive of its own (1.5x to 2.1x behind N6w where one bracket pass suffices), N1 as a primitive of its own (no cell). W4 (verified): the unrolled chunk loop (1.6x to 2.7x slower, T62), the run-relative swizzle (16-way conflicts at warp scope), the two-half per-lane sort (`W4_wmerge2`, 0.91x to 0.98x), the 4-ary merge path (`W4_chunk4q`, 0.84x and 0.89x), the warp merge at k 64 against W5_v4 (0.29x to 0.54x), the 8-run block merge for fp32 against the unsorted selection (0.82x) | opt-N8, opt-W5, opt-N10, opt-N5, opt-B4, opt-N6w, opt-W4, 2026-10-25, cards named in sections 17.4, 18.4, 19.4, 20.4, 21.4, 22.4 and 23.4 |

### 10.6 Node protocol and node facts

The swarm directory and its key files are listed in section 7. The node pool belongs to the broker, workers only take and return GPU leases, everything is file based under `$SWARM/state/` (gitignored) so it survives agent restarts, and `$SWARM/nodes/README.md` is the protocol. In five lines:

1. Registry `state/nodes.json`: one entry per node with name, cluster, arch, ssh target, job id, lease expiry, status, on-node root, `exec_prefix`, CUDA and nvcc version, clone commit and GPUs with their holder. Only the broker edits node entries, `tools/lease.py` changes only the holder fields under `fcntl.flock`. Hard cap six nodes across both clusters, leases of four hours as detachable `sbatch` holds (`nodes/hold.sh`), re-requested ahead of expiry, released with `scancel` after 30 idle minutes with no wave pending.
2. Lease: `python3 $SWARM/tools/lease.py acquire --holder <name> --arch any|b200|gb200` prints one line `node=... gpu=... root=... exec_prefix='...' arch=... cuda=... expires=... login=...` (parse it with `shlex.split`), `release --holder <name>` frees it. Hold a GPU only while a batch runs, never while compiling or thinking.
3. On the node: work in `<root>/work/<holder>/`, compile outside the lock, measure inside `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=<i> flock <root>/locks/gpu<i>.lock -c '<variant and control in one command>'`, write JSON results under `<root>/results/<holder>/`, one ssh per step, log to files and read the tail (long remote output has triggered truncation and classifier stops before).
4. Fetch right after every batch, nodes disappear every four hours: `python3 $SWARM/tools/broker.py fetch <holder>` tars `<root>/results/<holder>/` from every ready node into `$SWARM/state/results/<holder>/` and keeps the archives under `state/results/_archives/`.
5. Node facts verified in phase 0: both containers expose only the job's GPU, so the CUDA index is 0 and `locks/gpu0.lock` is always right. computelab B200 (x86, login `ssh computelab-sc-01`): docker container `topk_swarm` from `nvidia/cuda:13.4.2-devel-ubuntu24.04`, `exec_prefix` is `docker exec -i topk_swarm`, the host directory `/tmp/topk_swarm` is `/work`, cmake 4.3.2 copied from the NFS home. dlcluster GB200 (aarch64, login `ssh dlcluster`): the bare node has no docker daemon, no cmake and no CUDA toolkit, and `/raid` was not writable for the user, so the broker runs enroot directly (no pyxis, no slurm steps) with a persistent writable container `topk_swarm` from the same image, every command a fresh `enroot start` (about half a second, concurrent starts work), an `exec_prefix` that carries `ENROOT_RESTRICT_DEV=y`, `NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=<uuid>` and the `/tmp/estehle/topk_swarm:/work` mount, cmake 4.4.4 from a pip venv, and apt only through `enroot start --root` after `umount /etc/localtime`. Both containers have python3 3.12, git, ninja and flock and the clone at `<root>/cccl` on the swarm branch (update with `git fetch` and `git reset --hard origin/<branch>`, never commit there). Direct ssh from the Mac works on both clusters, the fallback is `ssh -J <login> <node>`. computelab slurm prints Pacific time and dlcluster UTC, registry times are UTC. The 5 GB NFS home is not a work area. Phase 0 nodes: umbriel-b200-020 (job 5288644, driver 615.71.10) and gb200-nvl4-ts2-92 (job 2819570, driver 595.84.01), both CUDA 13.4.92, leases to about 12:20 UTC on 2026-10-25. The reference cards were taken on the second pair, umbriel-b200-068 (job 5289760) and gb200-nvl4-ts2-100 (job 2821214), leases to about 13:50 and 13:58 UTC, and the broker now runs a tick loop with one job per host and container cleanup on release. Wave 1 ran on umbriel-b200-020, 047, 068 and 091 (B200) and gb200-nvl4-ts2-99 (GB200, opt-W1 and verify-W1 only), with the pool at the cap of six for most of the wave and leases rotating under the optimizers, which cost two ninety-second compiles under a held lease (W2) and the node mix behind the variance finding of 2.10.
6. Node fact added 2026-10-25 17:00 UTC (T50): umbriel-b200-020 runs fully unrolled kernels above about 64 KB of SASS at one warp per scheduler 1.29x to 1.33x slower than umbriel-b200-047 and 068, with every smaller kernel and every two-warp-per-scheduler kernel identical. A card whose kernel is in that class names its node and is measured on two nodes before a verdict, and the final evaluation runs on 047 or 068 or rolls such round loops. The two-node protocol opt-N8 used (same binaries, control and siblings in one lock section on each node, a 100 ms `nvidia-smi` trace alongside, `approaches/N8/node.sh` and `smi_summary.py`) is the template. Decision 17:55 UTC (T58): umbriel-b200-020 is retired from the pool after the hardware researcher's probes and excluded from the final evaluation, the three other B200 nodes measured (018, 047, 068) and 091 agree with each other. The cluster drained the host right after the last hold ended ("Stedge startup failed, reboot timed out"). The broker gained request by nodelist and the lease tool rejects option abbreviations after a typo created a stray lease. Phase 3 pre-flights (T58 updated): the two GB200 hosts gb200-nvl4-ts2-99 and ts2-105 agree within 1.6 percent on 25 cells and both run the 128-thread k 64 configuration with 32 to 64 items per thread 3 to 15 percent slower than the healthy B200s, every other 128-thread cell within 0.5 percent, so the GB200 pass values stand as measured, the opt-N6w GB200 cards carry no node caveat, and umbriel-b200-020 stays the only node outlier of the study.
7. Phase 3 protocol (2026-10-25 19:55 UTC): one dedicated healthy B200 other than umbriel-b200-020 after a hardware pre-flight, harness 0.10 frozen (its effects on the numbers are in 2.10 and `harness/README.md` section 3.1), every adapter against its scope's reference in the same lock section with canaries first and last of every batch, the scripts generated from the manifest by `tools/phase3.py` and reduced by `tools/winners.py`, then a GB200 pass and phase 3b.

## 11. W1, bitonic network top-k at warp and sub-warp scope (wave 1)

Conventions for sections 11 to 23. Unless a row says otherwise, numbers are B200, CUDA 13.4.92 (nvcc 13.4.92), `-arch=sm_100`, 2026-10-25, random input (bf16 rounded from the unit normal), requirement `not_guaranteed`, latency in cycles per call net of the generate-only control (chain slope), throughput in Gelem/s for one occupancy wave, variant and its control in one lock section, card files under `$SWARM/cards/` named without the `.json` suffix. "Verified" means the named verifier rebuilt the adapter from the card's commit, reran variant and control in one lock section on the named node and reproduced the card within the stated tolerance. "Unverified" means no verifier has run yet. "Pending verification" marks the wave 2 cards whose verifier was dispatched on 2026-10-25 (verify-N8, verify-W5, verify-N10) and had not written a verdict into the card when the section was written, except where the card already carries one. Reports: `$SWARM/approaches/<id>/REPORT*.md`.

### 11.1 What it is

The register-resident bitonic network of PR #12222 (`cub::detail::WarpBitonicTopK`, eager form: sort the first MaxK tile, then for every further tile sort it, keep the elementwise larger and merge) and its variants, striped over a logical warp of 4 to 32 lanes, shuffles only, no shared memory, no barrier, output sorted. Two optimizer instances ran W1 after a duplicate launch. opt-W1 (GB200 gb200-nvl4-ts2-99) measured the index-in-key words through the CUB network (`W1_packed`), its own level-parallel network with a `VIMNMX` exchange and template recursion (`W1_level`, filed as the N2 cards) and the sub-warp forms. opt-W1b (B200 umbriel-b200-020) measured one network template over element policies: the packed words (`W1_eager_same`), the level order on identical code (`W1_level_same`, `W1_level_pairs`), native fp32 min max pairs (`W1_minmax_pairs`), the unchanged CUB primitive with a struct comparator (`W1_baseline` short-circuit, `W1_kvcmp` branch-free) and the sub-warp forms of each. Fifteen cards, thirteen verified (verify-W1b reproduced every latency value to the cycle, verify-W1 the same on GB200), the two reference cards unverified.

### 11.2 Contract

Input N keys striped over the logical warp in registers (position i times LW plus lane), N a multiple of LW, the level form needs N over MaxK a power of two. MaxK is k rounded up to LW (W1b: to the next power of two, at least LW), k below MaxK pays the MaxK network. Keys bf16 (one 32-bit word: twiddled key high, inverted 16-bit index low, N at most 65536) or fp32 (u64 word with the inverted 32-bit index, or native pairs). Output: the k source indices in the caller's array, sorted by key descending and index ascending for the packed words, both requirements at the same cost for the packed words, `not_guaranteed` only for the pairs. Keys never materialized. No temp storage, shuffles inside the logical warp only. Minus zero canonicalized to plus zero before packing. NaN: the packed words order a NaN by its sign bit (deterministic), the pairs let a NaN lose every exchange. Partial tiles not in these prototypes.

### 11.3 Where the time goes

- A stage at one item per lane is one 30-cycle shuffle plus one or two 4 to 6 cycle ALU steps: 35 cycles for fp32 min max pairs (531 for the 15 stages of N 32), 38 for the packed u32, 39 for the pairs reference, 40 for the packed u64 (verified, `W1_minmax_pairs_b200_20261025_1` and siblings). Below that only fewer dependent stages help.
- The exchange: the bf16 u32 word costs `ISETP.GT.U32` plus `SEL` in the CUB comparator path (ptxas does not emit `VIMNMX` for a bool comparator on u32 words, contrary to issue #12160's integer remark) and two predicated `VIMNMX` in opt-W1's network. The u64 word costs two shuffles, `ISETP`, `ISETP.EX` and two `SEL`. The pairs reference costs two shuffles, two compares, `PLOP3`, two `SEL` and, for bf16, pack and unpack per stage, which is why bf16 pairs need 24 to 57 percent more SASS than fp32 pairs (10.4).
- Above one item per lane the eager loop's tile sorts are independent and ptxas overlaps them with the merge chain: packed bf16 costs 574, 761, 1211, 2371 cycles at N 32, 64, 128, 256 for k at most 32 on B200, 190 to 1160 cycles per added tile (verified). On GB200 through the CUB network the eager cost per added MaxK tile is about 710 to 740 cycles (fp32 990 at N 64 to 2408 at N 128).
- Instruction counts of the N 128 k 32 latency kernels (two copies of the network): packed bf16 728 (150 SHFL), packed fp32 1176 (300 SHFL), min max pairs 1136 (300 SHFL, 306 FMNMX), reference fp32 1152, reference bf16 1656 (424 HSETP2, 176 PLOP3), CUB with the short-circuit struct comparator 3448 (300 BSSY, 300 BSYNC, 305 BRA), branch-free 1840.
- Registers: packed bf16 20 to 27 at warp N 32 to 128, fp32 u64 24 to 42, the level network 35 to 64 at N 64 to 256, no spills at warp scope. At sub-warp LW 4 N 128 k 64 the fp32 min max pairs latency kernel spills 352 bytes at 168 registers and the bf16 one 48 bytes (the W1b report's "no spills" sentence is wrong for these two cells, corrected by verify-W1b).

|---|---|---|

Card `W1_packed_gb200_20261025_1` and `_sub`, verified by verify-W1 on gb200-nvl4-ts2-99 to the cycle, psi cells upgraded to a real control in the rerun |
| bf16 index in the key word, own network template (`W1_eager_same`, B200) | N 32 574 against 779 (1.36x), N 64 k 32 761 against 1164, N 128 k 16 1211 against 2267 (1.87x), N 256 k 32 2371 against 5640 (2.38x), N 256 k 8 and 16 2.51x. Throughput 254 against 125 Gelem/s at N 32, 263 against 102 at N 256 (2.0x to 2.7x), half the shuffles, 728 against 1656 instructions | adopted, the W1 answer for bf16 and for the tie-break requirement. Attribution of the gap (exp-W1codegen, card `W1x_codegen_b200_20261025_1`, B200 umbriel-b200-047, commit babb055d8a of `exp/topk-swarm/W1x`, one lease, five consecutive lock sections with `W1_packed` as the control in each, identical to the cycle in all five, bf16 u32 word, `not_guaranteed`, oracle 13 cases per cell, verified by verify-W1x: all six adapters rebuilt from babb055d8a and rerun on umbriel-b200-020, every latency cell to the cycle, throughput within 0.13 percent, zero SASS opcode mismatches in 48 kernels, and the three recursion kernels plus the CUB control rerun on gb200-nvl4-ts2-105 with the GB200 statements to the cycle). Five kernels on the same word, one factor changed at a time: (a) `W1_packed`, the CUB header, (e) `W1x_cub_eager`, the CUB exchange on opt-W1's recursion in eager order, (b) `W1x_cub_level`, the same in level order, (c) `W1x_vimnmx_eager`, the min max exchange in eager order, (d) `W1_level`, the min max exchange in level order. Latency in cycles: (a) 1205, 1267, 2417, 2965, (c) 1017, 912, 1718, 1322, (d) 883, 916, 1115, 1419 at N 128 k 32, N 128 k 64, N 256 k 32, N 256 k 64. The factors multiply within a few percent (1.14 x 1.39 x 1.37 is 2.17 at N 256 k 32).

| Factor, the other two held fixed | N 128 k 32 | N 128 k 64 | N 256 k 32 | N 256 k 64 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| exchange, min and max against compare and select, eager order (e over c) | 1.15x | 1.24x | 1.23x | 1.59x |
| exchange, level order (b over d) | 1.15x | 1.17x | 1.37x | 1.27x |
| tile order, level against eager, min max exchange, identical opcode histograms (c over d) | 1.15x | 1.00x | 1.54x | 0.93x |
| code structure, CUB header against the recursion, same algorithm and exchange (a over e) | 1.03x | 1.12x | 1.14x | 1.41x |
| all three, CUB header against opt-W1's level network (a over d) | 1.36x | 1.38x | 2.17x | 2.09x |
| exchange and structure, CUB header against the eager loop with the min max exchange (a over c), the route | 1.18x | 1.39x | 1.41x | 2.24x |
| aarch64 build of the CUB header, GB200 against B200, same source (T20) | 1.26x | 1.26x | 1.17x | 1.19x |

Throughput (256 threads, 8 blocks per SM for every kernel): the exchange is 1.10x to 1.24x, the tile order 0.96x to 1.01x, the code structure 0.99x to 1.09x, so in the ALU-issue-bound regime only the exchange pays (it removes about 1.7 `ISETP` and 1 `SEL` per stage and adds 2 `VIMNMX`, 104 to 160 instructions fewer per network copy). The tile order pays only with one word per tile (k 32: 1.15x at four tiles, 1.54x at eight, close to the dependency-depth model of idea N2) and loses 7 percent at two words per tile and four tiles (a T28 schedule effect, the chain is shorter). The recursion kernels compile to the same SASS and measure the same cycles on GB200, the CUB header does not (1521, 1598, 2840, 3539 cycles there), which is why opt-W1's GB200 ratios (1.72x to 2.55x) exceed the B200 ratios of the same pair (1.36x to 2.17x). opt-W1b's `W1_eager_same` sits with the CUB header in every cell (1211, 1338, 2371, 2800), and its level reorder changed the order alone inside an implementation whose phase 1 did not expose the overlap, which is why R12 was killed on identical code.

The bf16 route at warp scope, from this card: the min and max exchange on the packed u32 word (T42) in the recursion form, with the eager loop as the one-code-path answer at 1.18x to 2.24x over `WarpBitonicTopK` on B200 at the same registers as the level form and without the power-of-two tile count requirement. Where k is at most 32, N is at least 4 MaxK and latency matters, the level order adds 1.15x (N 128) to 1.54x (N 256) on top at equal throughput, the one corner in which R12 is reopened. At k 64 the eager loop is the better one (912 against 916, 1322 against 1419). For the CUB primitive itself this means a min max customization point next to the comparator, which the comparator form cannot express.

### 11.5 Ideas not yet tried

- fp32 keys-only network (one 32-bit shuffle and one `FMNMX` per stage) with index recovery by a ballot rank pass over the input, which gives `prefer_smaller_index` for free: idea card N10 (wave 2b), modeled at about the bf16 cost plus 200 cycles at N 128 against 1131 with the u64 word. Built and measured by opt-N10 as section 19 (verified by verify-N10 on a second node): the network runs on the twiddled u32 word with the min and max exchange at exactly the bf16 word's cost, the recovery costs 58 to 387 cycles at IPT 1 to 8, and the whole call is 960 against 1918 for the u64 word at N 128 k 16.
- The predicate operand of `FMNMX` and `IMNMX` for `keep_min ? min : max` in one instruction, no CUDA C or PTX form found.
- Two rows per lane in bf16x2 (N5, wave 2b) for the throughput regime, where the bf16 network is ALU bound. Built and measured as section 20 (verified): 1.22x to 1.44x per call over the one-word min and max network at k at most 32 and 1.43x to 1.82x at k 64 in one-wave throughput, 0.6x to 0.75x per call in latency at warp scope, a latency win at sub-warp scope from N 64 on.
- A level loop that pairs adjacent registers so consumed tiles retire early (the pairs latency gain without the register growth), and a two-half phase 1 at LW 4 for occupancy.
- The buffered variant of PR #9281 for N at least 8 MaxK, random and sorted input. Not built, the measured level network already removes most of the eager chain cost it targets.
- A `prefer_larger_index` form (index not inverted, one instruction less per item). Narrower tiles than LW for k below LW were modeled as no gain and not built.

### 11.6 Determinism and ordering

Wave 2 addendum (section 19, verified by verify-N10 on umbriel-b200-068): for fp32 the `prefer_smaller_index` route of the family is now N10's keys-only network with ballot rank recovery, which costs the same under both requirements and is faster than the min max pairs under `not_guaranteed` at every warp cell (0.99x to 0.27x) and at every sub-warp cell except LW 4 N 32 k 4 and 8 and N 8. Its output is in class and index order, not sorted, so the sorted-output pass stays with the u64 word or the pairs.

Inherent in every variant: deterministic and sorted output. The measured price of `prefer_smaller_index`: zero for the bf16 packed word (identical instantiation and identical cycles in all 52 warp and 76 sub-warp cell pairs on B200, within 0.13 percent on GB200), zero for the fp32 u64 word relative to itself but 13 percent at N 32, 24 to 30 percent at N 64, 16 percent at N 128 k at most 16 and 82 percent at N 128 k 64 against the min max pairs (and a gain of 11 to 16 percent at N 256 k at most 32), plus 9 to 10 percent throughput everywhere. Through the unchanged CUB primitive with a struct comparator: 2x to 7x short-circuit, 1.03x to 1.5x branch-free. Minus zero and plus zero compare equal under the exact requirement (signed_zero battery pattern).

- fp32 route after wave 2 (verified by verify-N10 on umbriel-b200-068, every headline latency to the cycle, the exact tie recovery confirmed by reading): N10 (section 19) replaces the u64 word under `prefer_smaller_index` and the pairs under `not_guaranteed` at warp scope for N 32 to 256 (661 against 934 at N 64 k 32, 960 against 1918 at N 128 k 16, 1341 against 4650 at N 256 k 64) and at sub-warp scope from N 32 (LW 8 and 16) or N 128 (LW 4). The u64 word keeps the sorted-output pass and N 8. The fp32 run of the codegen experiment (the u64 word's structure and order factors) is still owed, the N10 controls `N10_ctrl_u64rec` (the u64 word in the same recursion, 0.77x to 0.37x of it) and `W1x_vimnmx_eager` (the same u32 network with the index packed, the network-only cost) already separate the code structure from the keys-only gain.

Both instances were right about what they held fixed. Open from that card: why the level order loses 7 percent at two words per tile and four tiles (a schedule dump, T28), whether interleaving phase 1 stage by stage in opt-W1b's loop recovers the one-word order gain, the fp32 u64 word (no 64-bit `VIMNMX`, so its exchange factor is zero and opt-W1's 2.1x to 2.65x fp32 gap on GB200 must be structure, order and the aarch64 control, one more hour with keys fp32), and what a min max customization point in `cub::detail::WarpBitonicTopK` looks like. The card is verified by verify-W1x on second hosts of both archs, with two text caveats: the opcode histograms of the min max eager and level kernels are identical in six of eight cells but differ at N 256 k 64 (37 against 40 `ISETP`, 44 against 45 `LOP3` in the latency kernel, 800 against 752 instructions in the throughput kernel), so the 0.93x tile-order cell is not a pure schedule effect, and the per-stage model of about 36 against 44 cycles is a description and not a fit (the measured difference per dependent stage is about 5 cycles at N 128 k 32 and about 20 at N 256 k 64).
- T28: the latency of an unchanged network moves by up to 25 percent with k at fixed MaxK (an epilogue predicate), seen on the min max pairs network (1649 against 2004 at N 128) and on the CUB comparator network (`W1_packed` bf16 N 64 1150 at k 16 against 905 at k 32). Rule adopted: measure every (N, k) separately and quote the conservative control.
- No same-session comparison with W2 at warp N 64 to 256, k 16 to 64. Section 16 ranks across cards, the final evaluation will settle it.
- opt-W1 measured on GB200 only and opt-W1b on B200 only. Never compare the two across hosts (T20). GB200 was not measured for the W1b slice.
- The LW 16 fp32 route (min max 0.89x to 0.93x) and the N 128 k 32 pairs swing are schedule effects, a rescheduled build would settle them.  (cards `W1_minmax_pairs_sub` and `W1_minmax_pairs`, 11.4).
- The harness reports spill bytes only in the card schema, two sub-warp cells spill (11.3) and the W1b report says otherwise.
